Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in, I really appreciate it. Um, well, I guess what finally made me schedule an appointment is... and it's probably not a huge deal, I hope I'm not wasting your time, but... I just... I keep getting up when I know I’m really supposed to stay seated. I mean, I try to make it look completely normal! I'll just go refill my water glass, or maybe pace over by the printer for a second so I'm not bothering anyone. Um, well, I wouldn't say it's been forever. I mean, I was always pretty good about sitting still in school and everything, I promise! It's really just been since I took on this new role in advertising sales strategy... which was about two years and two months ago, roughly.

I've been having a little trouble during our long sales strategy meetings at work. \n\nI just... I keep getting up when I know I’m really supposed to stay seated. I mean, I try to make it look completely normal! I'll just go refill my water glass, or maybe pace over by the printer for a second so I'm not bothering anyone. It's just a bit embarrassing, I guess, since I should be able to just sit through a meeting like everyone else without needing to move around. Does that... does that make sense as a reason to be here?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this need to get up and move around in other settings too, or mainly during long meetings at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I think it's really just those long meetings at work. When I'm out doing everyday things, or even meeting with clients, I'm totally fine! I definitely don't want to make it sound like I can't sit through a dinner or a movie or anything like that. I'm not... I'm not bouncing off the walls everywhere I go. \n\nIt's just, you know, in ad sales I'm usually pretty active, so being stuck in a chair for a three-hour strategy session is just... well, I think anyone would get a little antsy, right? So, yeah, I haven't really noticed feeling that way in other settings. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: In those long meetings, do you find yourself feeling worried or anxious much of the time?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. I mean, I hope I'm not giving off that impression! It's not like I'm sitting there stressing out or having a panic attack or anything. \n\nI'm actually a pretty upbeat person most of the time. It's really just that physical feeling of needing to stretch my legs when I've been sitting for too long. I'm definitely not sitting there worrying about my performance or the presentation... I just get a little restless, that's all. Does that make sense? I just want to be sure I'm answering your questions the right way.\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness and need to get up been something you've had for a long time, or is it new?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I wouldn't say it's been forever. I mean, I was always pretty good about sitting still in school and everything, I promise! It's really just been since I took on this new role in advertising sales strategy... which was about two years and two months ago, roughly. \n\nBefore that, I was out in the field a lot more, so I guess I didn't really notice it. It's only since I've had to be in these really long, intensive strategy meetings that it's become a bit of a... well, a little noticeable to me. I hope that's not too weird? Like, is it normal for something like that to just pop up when your job changes? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or unable to sit still in situations outside of those long meetings, even when you’re not at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Like I said before, I think I'm pretty good outside of those meetings. I mean, I can go to a restaurant with friends or watch a movie at home without feeling like I need to jump out of my seat. I'm definitely not one of those people who can't relax! \n\nI really just think it's the environment of those strategy sessions, you know? Being cooped up for so long. Outside of that, I'm totally fine. I hope I'm being clear enough about that? I don't want you to think I'm struggling all the time or anything. I'm doing really well in most areas of my life, I promise!\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I'm really not a worrier at all, honestly! I don't want to come across as someone who's always anxious or overwhelmed, because I'm really not. I handle things pretty well, I think. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how I am on a day-to-day basis.\n\nDoctor: Have these meetings started causing problems for you at work?\n\nPatient: Um... well... It honestly made me feel awful, because I care so, so much about doing good work for them. It just made me worry that I could accidentally damage my accounts, even though I'm trying my absolute best to stay focused. I'm still working really hard, I promise! I hope it's okay that I'm sharing that... I just really don't want you to think I'm slacking off or not taking my job seriously, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's only since I've had to be in these really long, intensive strategy meetings that it's become a bit of a... well, a little noticeable to me. I hope that's not too weird? Like, is it normal for something like that to just pop up when your job changes? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or unable to sit still in situations outside of those long meetings, even when you’re not at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I'm definitely not one of those people who can't relax! \n\nI really just think it's the environment of those strategy sessions, you know? Being cooped up for so long. Outside of that, I'm totally fine. I hope I'm being clear enough about that? I don't want you to think I'm struggling all the time or anything. I'm doing really well in most areas of my life, I promise!\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I'm not bouncing off the walls everywhere I go. \n\nIt's just, you know, in ad sales I'm usually pretty active, so being stuck in a chair for a three-hour strategy session is just... well, I think anyone would get a little antsy, right? So, yeah, I haven't really noticed feeling that way in other settings. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: In those long meetings, do you find yourself feeling worried or anxious much of the time?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. I mean, I hope I'm not giving off that impression! It's not like I'm sitting there stressing out or having a panic attack or anything. \n\nI'm actually a pretty upbeat person most of the time. It's really just that physical feeling of needing to stretch my legs when I've been sitting for too long. I'm definitely not sitting there worrying about my performance or the presentation... I just get a little restless, that's all. Does that make sense? I just want to be sure I'm answering your questions the right way.\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness and need to get up been something you've had for a long time, or is it new?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I wouldn't say it's been forever. I mean, I was always pretty good about sitting still in school and everything, I promise! It's really just been since I took on this new role in advertising sales strategy... which was about two years and two months ago, roughly. \n\nBefore that, I was out in the field a lot more, so I guess I didn't really notice it. It's only since I've had to be in these really long, intensive strategy meetings that it's become a bit of a... well, a little noticeable to me. I hope that's not too weird? Like, is it normal for something like that to just pop up when your job changes? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or unable to sit still in situations outside of those long meetings, even when you’re not at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Like I said before, I think I'm pretty good outside of those meetings. I mean, I can go to a restaurant with friends or watch a movie at home without feeling like I need to jump out of my seat. I'm definitely not one of those people who can't relax! \n\nI really just think it's the environment of those strategy sessions, you know? Being cooped up for so long. Outside of that, I'm totally fine. I hope I'm being clear enough about that? I don't want you to think I'm struggling all the time or anything. I'm doing really well in most areas of my life, I promise!\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I'm really not a worrier at all, honestly! I mean, I have the normal everyday stuff, like, 'Did I send that email?' or 'What's for dinner?' but I'm not sitting around stressing about things most days. \n\nI try to keep a pretty positive outlook on life, you know? I don't want to come across as someone who's always anxious or overwhelmed, because I'm really not. I handle things pretty well, I think. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how I am on a day-to-day basis.\n\nDoctor: Have these meetings started causing problems for you at work?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I really hate to admit this, because I pride myself on being a good employee, I really do! But... I guess it has started to be a little bit of an issue. It actually came up in my recent performance review... just a note about maintaining client trust during those really long presentations. \n\nAnd, um... there was actually one specific client complaint recently. It honestly made me feel awful, because I care so, so much about doing good work for them. It just made me worry that I could accidentally damage my accounts, even though I'm trying my absolute best to stay focused. I'm still working really hard, I promise! I hope it's okay that I'm sharing that... I just really don't want you to think I'm slacking off or not taking my job seriously, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this restlessness mainly happens because the meetings themselves are stressful or demanding?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not! I really don't want it to sound like I can't handle the pressure, because I actually thrive on it. The work itself isn't too demanding at all—I really love what I do, and I'm good at the strategy part! \n\nIt's just... I don't know, just the physical act of being stuck in one chair for three hours straight without a break. It's really not the stress of the meeting or the clients, just the sitting part. Does that make sense? I just want to be sure you know I'm completely capable of handling the actual work! I'd hate for you to think I'm getting overwhelmed by the job itself.\n\nDoctor: Do these meetings cause you significant distress or problems outside of work, like affecting your mood or your ability to enjoy the rest of your day?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no! I'm usually pretty upbeat, and I definitely still enjoy my evenings and weekends. I wouldn't want you to think I'm, like, bringing all this negativity home with me or letting it affect my personal life, because I'm really good at leaving work at work, you know? I just need to stretch my legs a bit and then I'm totally back to normal. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information!\n\nDoctor: Do these long meetings feel stressful to you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not stressful at all! I mean, I really hope I'm not making it sound like I can't handle my job, because I actually really like the fast pace of advertising. The meetings themselves are fine! I'm totally comfortable talking to clients and going over the numbers. \n\nIt's really just... the sitting still part. My brain just kind of wants to go, go, go, and my body wants to move. I really want to make sure I'm explaining this right so you don't think I'm just easily overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: When you’re sitting in those meetings, do you feel an uncomfortable inner urge that goes away once you get up and move?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess maybe a little bit? And yes, once I stand up and walk down the hall for a coffee, I feel completely fine again. It's really no big deal, honestly. I hope I'm not making too much out of it—does that sound like a normal thing to feel when you've been sitting for that long? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right idea.\n\nDoctor: Before these work meetings started, did you have similar trouble sitting still as a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, well... And, okay, my desk was usually just stuffed with half-finished papers. But I was just really enthusiastic, you know? I wanted to participate in everything! I really hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker. I was always very polite to my teachers! Is that... is that the kind of childhood stuff you're looking for? I want to make sure I'm answering this the right way.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar problems with attention, restlessness, or impulsivity in more than one setting growing up, like at home as well as at school?\n\nPatient: Um, at home? I really don't want to make it sound like I'm crawling out of my skin or that I can't control myself. \n\nIt's more just... a slight restlessness, I suppose? Like my legs just want to stretch. And yes, once I stand up and walk down the hall for a coffee, I feel completely fine again. It's really no big deal, honestly. I hope I'm not making too much out of it—does that sound like a normal thing to feel when you've been sitting for that long? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right idea.\n\nDoctor: Before these work meetings started, did you have similar trouble sitting still as a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, well... I guess I did? But I promise I wasn't a bad kid or anything! I actually got really good grades, and I loved school. I just... well, my elementary school teachers used to write on my report cards that I was, um, bright but that I rushed through things. \n\nI guess I did talk out of turn sometimes, and maybe I forgot my homework here and there. And, okay, my desk was usually just stuffed with half-finished papers. But I was just really enthusiastic, you know? I wanted to participate in everything! Well... I mean, I guess maybe a little? But my parents were always really happy with me, I promise! We had a great relationship. \n\nI suppose my mom used to say I was a bit of a whirlwind, always bouncing from one activity to the next. And I did help out around the house, even if she sometimes had to remind me a few times to finish putting my clothes away because I'd get distracted by a book or something. \n\nBut I was a good daughter, really! People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, maybe occasionally? But I really try not to be rude! I know how important it is to listen, especially in my line of work. I promise I don't mean to be disrespectful or talk over anyone. 

It's just that sometimes... well, if a client or a coworker is explaining something, I get so excited because I think I know exactly what they need or what they're going to ask. So I might, um, sort of jump in with an answer before they've completely finished their sentence. And then sometimes I realize I actually answered the wrong thing because I didn't wait to hear the whole question. 

But I always apologize right away! I just get really eager to help them, you know? I want them to know I'm on top of things. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible listener. I really do care about what they're saying! Is that... is that the kind of thing you're asking about? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.

I'm not bouncing off the walls everywhere I go. \n\nIt's just, you know, in ad sales I'm usually pretty active, so being stuck in a chair for a three-hour strategy session is just... well, I think anyone would get a little antsy, right? So, yeah, I haven't really noticed feeling that way in other settings. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: In those long meetings, do you find yourself feeling worried or anxious much of the time?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or worried. I mean, I hope I'm not giving off that impression! It's not like I'm sitting there stressing out or having a panic attack or anything. \n\nI'm actually a pretty upbeat person most of the time. It's really just that physical feeling of needing to stretch my legs when I've been sitting for too long. I'm definitely not sitting there worrying about my performance or the presentation... I just get a little restless, that's all. Does that make sense? I just want to be sure I'm answering your questions the right way.\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness and need to get up been something you've had for a long time, or is it new?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I wouldn't say it's been forever. I mean, I was always pretty good about sitting still in school and everything, I promise! It's really just been since I took on this new role in advertising sales strategy... which was about two years and two months ago, roughly. \n\nBefore that, I was out in the field a lot more, so I guess I didn't really notice it. It's only since I've had to be in these really long, intensive strategy meetings that it's become a bit of a... well, a little noticeable to me. I hope that's not too weird? Like, is it normal for something like that to just pop up when your job changes? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or unable to sit still in situations outside of those long meetings, even when you’re not at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Like I said before, I think I'm pretty good outside of those meetings. I mean, I can go to a restaurant with friends or watch a movie at home without feeling like I need to jump out of my seat. I'm definitely not one of those people who can't relax! \n\nI really just think it's the environment of those strategy sessions, you know? Being cooped up for so long. Outside of that, I'm totally fine. I hope I'm being clear enough about that? I don't want you to think I'm struggling all the time or anything. I'm doing really well in most areas of my life, I promise!\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a lot of different things most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I'm really not a worrier at all, honestly! I mean, I have the normal everyday stuff, like, 'Did I send that email?' or 'What's for dinner?' but I'm not sitting around stressing about things most days. \n\nI try to keep a pretty positive outlook on life, you know? I don't want to come across as someone who's always anxious or overwhelmed, because I'm really not. I handle things pretty well, I think. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how I am on a day-to-day basis.\n\nDoctor: Have these meetings started causing problems for you at work?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I really hate to admit this, because I pride myself on being a good employee, I really do! But... I guess it has started to be a little bit of an issue. It actually came up in my recent performance review... just a note about maintaining client trust during those really long presentations. \n\nAnd, um... there was actually one specific client complaint recently. I hope it's okay that I'm sharing that... I just really don't want you to think I'm slacking off or not taking my job seriously, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this restlessness mainly happens because the meetings themselves are stressful or demanding?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not! I really don't want it to sound like I can't handle the pressure, because I actually thrive on it. Does that make sense? I just want to be sure you know I'm completely capable of handling the actual work! I'd hate for you to think I'm getting overwhelmed by the job itself.\n\nDoctor: Do these meetings cause you significant distress or problems outside of work, like affecting your mood or your ability to enjoy the rest of your day?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no! No, definitely not. I mean, once the meeting is over and I can finally get up and move around, I'm completely fine! I bounce right back. \n\nI really don't let it ruin my day or anything like that. I'm usually pretty upbeat, and I definitely still enjoy my evenings and weekends. I wouldn't want you to think I'm, like, bringing all this negativity home with me or letting it affect my personal life, because I'm really good at leaving work at work, you know? I just need to stretch my legs a bit and then I'm totally back to normal. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information!\n\nDoctor: Do these long meetings feel stressful to you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not stressful at all! I mean, I really hope I'm not making it sound like I can't handle my job, because I actually really like the fast pace of advertising. The meetings themselves are fine! I'm totally comfortable talking to clients and going over the numbers. \n\nIt's really just... the sitting still part. My brain just kind of wants to go, go, go, and my body wants to move.
